U. S.-to Fight
Japan Soon'
PORTLAND, Ore., Feb. 17 (A.P.)
—Upton Close, Oriental affairs ex-
pert, says the United States will "be
forced to fight Japan soon" as the
result of "an economic war."
Close, whose real name is Josef
Washington Hall, went to China as
an intelligence officer at the out-
break of the World war. He adopt-
ed the pen name Upton Close in
writing a dozen books on the Far
East.
He declared the United States
was furnishing materials for Japan's
new battleships.
"Japan is getting supplies for her
world-challenging navy from this
country," said Close. "A month ago
she purchased 90,000 tons of pig
iron from Birmingham, Ala., for
would be the last large purchase
armor plate. The plant for rolling
of pig iron by Tokyo "because 
this iron is being built in Pittsburgh
expects to take possession of the
and shipped to Japan in sections."
great iron mines on the Yangts
Close predicted the shipment
river near Hankow."

Sir:
By reference from the White House, I aoknowledge the
receipt of your letter of February 21, l938, in regard to
the shipment of war and other materials to Japan.
There is transmitted herewith a oopy of the Joint
Resolution of Congress approved May 1, 1937, amending the
Joint Resolution approved August 31, 1935. You will ob-
serve that Seotion 1 (a) of this Joint Resolution states
that, whenever the President shall find that there exists
a state of war between or among two or more foreign states,
he shall proolaim suoh faot. Should the President issue
a proolamation finding that a state of war exists between
China and Japan, the export of arme, ammunition, and im-
plements of war to both those oountries would immediately
be prohibited by the terms of the Neutrality Act. In the
F/
absence
M

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
absence of sueh a proolamation, however, there exists no
legal authority for preventing the export of arms either
to both those countries or to Japan alone. The artioles
and materials whioh are considered to be arms, ammunition,
and implements of war are enumerated in the President's
Proolamation of May 1, l937, a copy of whioh is enolosed.
There are no statutes in existenoe whioh would author-
ize the President to prohibit the export of artioles other
than arms, ammunition, or implements of war with the emoep-
tion of the aots of February 15, 1936, and September 1,
1939, relating to the exportation of tin-plate sorap and
helium, respeotively.
I may add that the purpose of the Joint Resolution of
May 1, l937, is to keep this country out of war. The ques-
tion as to whether and when the provisions of the resolu-
tion shall be invoked is properly left to the sound dis-
oretion of the President. In deciding whether he should
invoke the resolution in a partioular situation, the Presi-
dent must take into aocount various factors and partioularly
the broad, general interests of the country and its nationals.
In the present situation in the Far East, the President
has conoluded that, in the light of all the faots and oir-
oumstances, when weighed from the praotical rather than
from a purely theoretioal or narrow teohnioal point of
view,

894.2
Hon. Franklin D. Roosevelt,
AH LASILAN ALIAINS
President of the United States,
The White House,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. President:
Today I had the privilege of lunching with Mr. George Fitch,
8
who has just returned from China via the China Clipper. He was at the
2
head of the relief movement and was present all through the siege of
Nanking, and he told me that the sacking of Magdeburg by Tilly was nothing
in comparison to the horrors and the brutality of the Japanese in and around
Nanking.
Is there not something that could be done to stop the supply
of war material which is at present flowing from the United States into
Japan? The country is filled with horror at the brutalities of the Japan-
ese and yet we are supplying them with the very munitions which enable them
to carry on this awful war against a peaceful people. It is all very well
to say that we must keep this country out of war, but it is worse to refuse
to help a friendly nation with whom we have been on the best of terms for
one hundred and fifty years.
This country will stand just so much, and i do not believe
that it will continue to act the roll of the priest and the Levite in our
treatment of China.
Hoping that you can find your way clear to do something in
the matter, I am
Yours very sincerely,
Hemy 1B. wombly
APR26193
F/WB

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.97)
Northern Life Tower
Seattle, Washington
April 8, 1938
Dear Pastor:
In view of the continued war in various parts of the world and
•speoially in Japan, many feel that Christian people should do something
in the name of decency to stop it, if possible, before the whole world is
in conflagration.
In a meeting on Tuesday, when representatives of many of the
leading churches, business men, and labor organizations were present, the
resolution given below was unanimously adopted. It was requested that it
be sent to various churches for signatures. Will you, therefore, please
type this resolution on your church stationery or blank stationery with
your name written in. Make a number of copies and appoint several young
people, who may be given the responsibility, to get as many signatures as
possible in tho morning and evening services next Sunday.
FORM TO BE USED
FIRST PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH
(Namo of the Church)
We the undersigned members of the Church
and congregation of (OF THE..FIRST PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH OF SEATTLE,
Washington do hereby earnestly appeal to the Pres
ident of the United States, State Department, the
Senate, and House of Representatives to hasten the
necessary legislation to prohibit the shipment of
sorap iron and munitions to any aggressor nation.
NA ?.a.DRESSL
. Ma be a motlo. ParTo..
If you will promptly mail them to me after Sunday, I shall send
them directly to President Roosevelt.
"While Rome burns" we can do something. It is known that such
resolutions as this have very great effect upon our representatives in
Washington.
Yours very truly,
Waeter G Hiltmen
Walter G. Hiltner
Chairman of Committee Against
International Aggression

found chemically unsatisfactory of late as a result of
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
the 30 percent sta le fiber content of all Japanese
cloth manufactured for domestic use since February 1,
OF STATE

1938.

This report, which has come to me from a large
Japanese chemical firm through the Mitsubishi Shoji,
indicates that the Japanese machinery for reducing
cotton cloth waste to nitro-cellulose for use in the
manufacture of explosives is equipped only for the
F/A
consumption of pure cotton waste. rhe report goes on to
state

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
state that the wood fiber found in the cloth mixture now
in production cannot be dissolved by the reagent now used,
and causes a chemical reaction which is deleterious to
the finished nitrate product. It is indiccted that this
problem is causine concern to Ju enece army euthorities
and chemical engineers.
It is considered unlikely, however, tiat this state
of affairs is serious enough to cause any change in
Japanese textile policy. Intensive study is being given
to the problem with & view to develoring e fesible pro-
cess which will enable the munitions industry to use
either pure or mixed cotton waste at will.
It is further reported that the staple fiber mixture
has been found unsuitavle clsewhere, particularly in the
Governnent railways system where the use of mixed waste
as packing in locomotive gear boxes has been found un-
satisfactory. However, this problem is distinctly secondary
to thet facing the munitions industry.

I heve the honor to transcribe below from Hansard
questions in the House of Commons and the answers of
the Parliamentary Secretary of the Board of Trade re-
garding exports of British war material to Japan:
"Mr. Riley asked the President of
the Board of Trade the number of licences
issued by the Government for the export
of war material to Japan since lst July,
1937, to the nearest available date, and
the value of such exports?
Captain Wallace: Eight licences for
the export of war material from the United
Kingdom/

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Kingdom to Japan have been issued since
1st July, 1937. The total value of the
arms, ammunition and military and naval
stores registered as consigned from the
United Kingdom to Japan from lst July,
1937, to 28th February, 1938, was £61,000.
Mr. Riley: In view of the fact that the
Government of Japan are carrying on an
aggressive war against a country which is
a member of the League of Nations, is it
consistent with the policy of the Govern-
ment and their membership of the League
to assist the export of war material to
an aggressor State?
Captain Wallace: Under the Anglo-Japanese
Treaty of 19ll no prohibition may be im-
posed on exports to Japan unless a similar
prohibition applies to all destinations.
Mr. Noel-Baker: Has the Minister over-
looked the fact that Treaties which are
not in accordance with the Covenant are
thereby null and void?"

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
COPY
Dav1son,M1ch.5/4/1938.
Honorable Andrew J. Transue, Washington, D. C.
Honorable and dear sir:-
I have been hoping against hope that the Honorable
President of these United States would invoke what I conoeive
to be his power, privilege and duty under the Neutrality law,
and stop the shipment of Scrap Iron, raw Cotton and Oil to
Japan, thus assisting in bringing to a close the awful slaughter
of human beings-many of them non-combatants- and the wicked
destruotion of property, imposed by an aggressor nation upon
an inoffensive neighbor in an utterly uncalled for and un-
deolared war.
In the name of God and humanity, will you not use your
influence to bring this about? I speak as a Retired min1ster
of the great Methodist Ohurch, as one who has sought through
the years to establish the conditions prophesied by the
Angelic chorus, when they announced at the birth of the Prince
of Peace, "Peace on earth and goodwill towards men".
Yours most respeotfully,
FRED H. TOWNSEND

JUL - 7 1938
DEPARTMENT UF SIATE
sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's telegram
196a
No. 139, April 20, 6 p.m., in which the Embassy is requested
to consider a statement in Kobe's despatch No. 235, Maroh
FILED
17, 1938, that Japan's military imports are not included
in official statistics, and to comment on and discuss the
present level and trend of suoh non-recorded imports as
F/A
compared with the period prior to the conflict with China.
As indicated in the enclosure to the Embassy's despatch
No. 2754, February 2, 1938, whatever may be considered to
be

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
be a military secret is safeguarded by legislation pro-
viding for drastic penalties, including capital punish-
ment and life sentence for those who coming into know-
ledge of military secrets in the conduct of their busi-
ness, reveal them to foreign countries. It will also
be noted that, among other items formerly listed in the
official trade returns, statistics with regard to the
importation of more than fifty metals, including iron,
copper, magnesium, chromium and nickel, are now omitted.
In addition, direct purchases from abroad by the armed
services, such as fuel oil and munitions, have never, so
far as can be ascertained, been included among the pub-
lished trade returns. The normal channels of information
being effectively closed, a search has been made of articles
in technical periodicals, in the hope that information
bearing on the question raised by the Department might
inadvertently have been made public, but thus far without
material success. The consulates were directed to furnish
the Embassy with whatever information on the subject might
be available, but Yokohama, Nagoya and Teihoku reported
that they were unable to furnish any information of value.
The consulate at Kobe has not as yet submitted a report.
In view of the circumstances above-described, it
will be appreciated that the information desired by the
Department is not readily procurable. It is possible
that by collection over an extended period of time of
fragmentary information, some indication of broad trends
1n

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
in imports of military supplies can be deduced. We
have begun to collect such information, and a report
will be submitted as soon as any information of value
is available. In the meantime, the Department may be
interested in the Military ttaché's estimate of stocks
of essential raw materials on hand as reported to the
War Department in his telegram No. 363 of January 14,
1938.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

ican people." As one of the 130 millions or so of the American
people, I would certainly like to do my part in remedying the
situation-- but what can I do except tell you and Mr. Hull and
Mr. Satterfield and Mr. Glass and Mr. Byrd what I think?
A smali group of us here in Richmand have for some time
constituted a local boycott committee, encouraging the boycott,
by consumers, of goods manufactured in Japan and of articles
manufactured here from raw silk produced in Japan. But we have
realized more and more that the situation really rests in the
hands of those who are making it possible for Japan to keep up
her aggression in China by supplying the oil, steel, nitrates,
JUN
et cetera which are indispensable to her military program.

1338

We have been particularly interested in connection with the war
whioh Japan is carrying on in China-- but Mr. Janeway's article
has served to give a somewhat broader base to a protest I had in
mind to make to Washington anyway.

F/A

Lindsay, Oklahoma
June 4, 1938.
Hon. Josh Lee, Senator,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Lee:
I have just heard the news broadcast of the awful bombing
of Hong Kong China, today, evidently the worst of this
useless butchering of helpless women and children.
The publications of the country give us the information
that shipments of all kinds of war materials are going
through to Japan in large quantities. That like shipments
to China are being held up. Can you ascertain for me,
Mr. Lee, if this is true?
Aside from this question, I, with many others, feel that it
is high time that our goveriment do more than merely pro-
test, although we appreciate the stand taken by Mr. Wells.
We feel that war supplies of all kinds should be cut off
to Japen, at least until she ceases this slaughter of the
innocent.
I respectfully request you to present my views, which are
not mine only, but held by thousands of others, to the
State Department, as you are our representative in Washing-
ton, and in position to bring this to the attentionof the
proper authorities.
Yours sincerely,
W. C. Sawyer

EMPORIA, KANSAS
June 11, 1938.
Senator Harry F. Byrd,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Byrd,
As a former resident of Virginia, and a
graduate of its State University, I am moved to write to
the Senator from virginia on a matter of deep concern.
Is there nothing our government and people can do to
hinder or prevent this awful slaughter of civilians in China?
The war-madness of Japan, in ruthless attack on a friendly and
peaceable neighbor, 1s aided and abetted by our sending oil,
scrap-iron, and munitions to the aggressor. In effect, we
become Japan's partner in ghastly destruotion. If our sup-
plies to the aggressor were to be out off, this ravaging of a
great and oivilized people would prforce cease. No loans
should be allowed to Japan for these nefarious purposes.
I respeotfully urge you to think and plan for any help
that may be given to the cause of justice and humanity 1n China.
Yours sincerely,
WARREN HORTON STUART.
(Professor of Religion, College of Emporia).

June 13, 1938.
Senator Robert J. Bulkley,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
As a citizen of these United States, may I ask you,
1n God's name, to stop the shipment of arms, and all other
war material to Japan from this country?
Surely the motives of humanity and decency should be
sufficlent, but if they are not, at least any one can see
that 1n helping Japan to win her nefarious course, we are
but arming a future rival and enemy. Once victorious,
Japan will put an end to the "open door" in China, and
with it, an end to our trade in the Orient.
Most of all we are behaving in a way 1nconsistent
with all decenoy and Christianity in turning our back
upon helpless China and helping her enemies.
Sincerely
MIRIAM L. ROGERS (M188)
(Teacher of Adult Education)

CONVSIO CF LD DIUM-SC.LE INDUSTRIES
TO RI:T PRODUCTION
It is natural that with the large companies fall-
ing continually behind in their production of war
supplies, a movement looking to the conversion of
small industries to munitions producticn, should
develop. Principel obetecles to this rocess of
conversion have been l ch cf c-pital, skilled labor,
and technical knovledge. It hus been felt thut the
quality standerds of the army nd Navy coule not be
met by small ftetories Working independently und
without sunervision. nxious to obtain this lucrative
bus iness, the industrial organizatiorr of the smaller
manuftcturers have been petitioning the Government
for recognition of their n ticuler oroblems.
In response to these demends, the inistry of
Commerce end Industry has announced th plications
for subsidies will be received from the various guilds
through the month of June. An appropriation of
¥1,600,000 is available and it is expected that the
industrial organizations will contribute an equal amount.
This money will be used in making changes in equipment
necessary for the production of armaments. The follow-
ing conditions have been set up:
1. Qualifications

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -

Receive Subsidies.
a.
Grants will be made to organizations
representing industries rel ted to
the armement industry, either from
the point of viev. of technique or
e uipnent. This will include not
only manufucturers Mho will supply
the rmy directly but also those ho
produce raw mterials or nachine tools
used in the manufacture of armaments.
E uipment for which Subsidy is to
be Used.
a. Instell tion of new equipment, increase
and renovttion of pre nt facilities.
b.
In addition to e uipment used directly
in production, funds may be used for
investietion, storage, transportetion,
or laborctory e uipment.
c.
quipment may be set up in one or nore
places for nutual use by members of
the uild or for rental to individual
members.
3. Percentage of Jubsidies und Time of
Payment.
as a general rule en amount equal to
50% of the necesstry expenses of 8
project will be grnted. In cases of
necessity, this percentage ray be
increased.
b.
When half or more of the expenses of
a project have been peid, the appro-
priation of the Government will be
available. *
A great many proposals have been made with regard
to the solution of the labor problem. The Osaka
Federation of Industrial and Commercial Unions recently
issued a long memorandum on the subject of industrial
education, recommending especially that students in
technical
*NIKKAN KOGYO SHIMBUN, Osaka, Japan, May 9, 1938.

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.98)

technical schools be given much more practical train-
ing, that more men with actual experience in industry
be selected as techers, thet industrial organizations
as well es the Government meke contributions to train-
ing institutions.*
In view of the fact that so m&ny highly skilled
workers have been drafted into the rny, the suggestion
has come from industrialists thet such skilled mechanics,
especially those vorkin in War industries, be exempted
from military service. .s fr us is known, no action
has been taken to brin: such a proposal before the
Governent.
One plan for improving the efficiency of snall
and medium scale inuustries in the production of
armaments has been presented by m&jor General atsuda,
Chief of the Tokyo Prefectural Institute for the
Encouregement of Industry. This plan, which is said
to have the support of the wilitary authorities, would
provide for the grouping of small factories in "blocks"
of ten or more. manufacturers within these "blocks"
would pool their equipment and resources so that the
advantages of large scale production might be gained.
It would also be possible to exchange specialists in
certain skilled operations.

* NIKKAN KOGYO SHIMBUN, Osaka, Japan, April 24, 1938.

In Tokyo an Industrial Supervisory Bureau has
been established. A comnittee of technical experts,

supervising the mnuft cture of war materials. It is
hoped thet the tller producers, benefitine from this
technical supervision, will be able to fill oruers

The ministry of Comuerce and Inuustry is announced

prefectural governnents Will be held in Toyo on

assist the smaller industries in their conversion to
rmament production. Concrete methods Wich can be put
into practice by prefectural authorities for inprove-
ment of technique, equipment, and training of workers,

In 0seka it was announced on April 19, 1938 that
twenty officers hed been newly employed by the prefectural
government, whose principal duties will be to aid the
smaller industries to more efficient armament production.
A representative of the Oseka Institute for the Encourage-
ment of Industry was recently sent to Kure for conferences
with naval technicians and it is expected that through
close cooperation with military authorities, the output
of the smaller producers can be vastly improved.

april 20, 193e: Two Osuke manuf cturers of print-

manuft cture printing eguipuent, but will

April 17, 1938: The Hitachi Company, manufacturers
of electric drills, grinders, and various types
of machine tools announce that due to the
continued increase of orders from the military
authorities as well as from other quarters
and the difficulty of obtaining raw materials,
they will in future refuse all orders except
those from the rmy and Navy.*
May 16, 1938: Following the action of the Hitachi
Company in refusing orders from private con-
cerns, itsubishi will in future supply
electric drills and grinders only to the
military authorities. Due to difficulties in
procuring raw materials and the copper
control, Mitsubishi has been reducing its
sales to private companies. Unfilled orders
now on hand from these concerns will be
canceled.*

IV. CONCLUSION
Conditions in Japan's industrial circles at the
present time indicate that the China Inciuent is
necessiteting wide chan es in the naticn's methods of
production und industrial organization. Une of the
unique feetures of Japan's economy has been her small
industries with their low wage standards and production
costs. The present loss of export business ond demand
for ermament production threetens thet system. Small
shops able to turn out cheap export articles cannot
easily change to making precise instruments for war.
There is a divergence of opinion among Japanese
econonists and industrialists as to the future of her
small industry system. It may be possible, through
Government subsidy and supervision, to control the
small industries through their unions, and keep them
as an integral part of the national economy. If,
however, the plen fails, the smell industries may be
unable to continue their existence. It is just
possible that their ability to produce munitions
effectively may be the determining factor.

and supplies, aviation material, etc. to Japan. I also said tha
894.24/214
it is becoming more and more clear to Americans that China is fighting
for the cause of liberty in the world as a whole.
Since June 13, there has been continued bombing
of American property in several parts of China and the papers,of
June 2l, carried an announcement that Japan desires all western nations
to recognize her plan to regard as a war zone all that part of China
lying east of a line from Sian to the Island of Hainan. Each day I
meet Americans who believe the State Department will not consent to any
such delimitation of zones in China. Their fear is that if the State
Department attempts to prevent shipment of goods to Japan, Japan will
retort by declaring war against China in the hope that the United States
and other countries will have to invoke neutrality legislation.
Will it not be possible for the State Department
to bring pressure on manufacturers, in one category at a time, so as
to prevent the shipment of cars and materials of war which are bringing
F/WB
such devastation into China and which Americans in China so deplore?

confidence in the Secretary of State and who believe that he and his
colleagues will find a way to diminish the devastating aspects of
Japan's invastion.

La Marque, Tx.,
June 18, 1938.
Senator Tom Connally,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator:
I am writing you to urge you to do all in your power to
stop the sale of bombing planes and other war material to
Japan. It seems a vicious thing that we as a nation can be
guilty of selling out the poor defenseless Chinese contribut-
1ng all in our power to further their destruction - for money.
On the other hand we are urged by President down to last
clergyman to contribute funds to charitable organization to
help alleviate the suffering of the Chinese. Truly we are
consistent in being inconsistent and we will be called upon
to answer for our greed!
Yours truly,
NELLIE M. BRODERSON.
(Mrs. H. J. B.)

1058.
Hon. Cordell Hull,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.
A
My dear Mr. Secretary:
Having lived in China for a great many years and also for a
period in Japan, I feel very deeply the present situation in the
Far-East. It seems to me that for us to be supplying the mater-
894.24/2
1als that the Japanese are using to destroy the Chinese nation is
contributing to an international outrage, and should, if possible,
be prevented. Is there nothing that can be done, which will not
involve us in a war with Japan, to prevent the shipments of large
quantities of oil, cotton, scrap iron, not to mention munitions
and armaments to Japan? The article in this month's Harpers' on
Japan's Partner should fill every right-minded American with
shame.
Is there any possibility of putting shipments to Japan on a
8
quota basis which would correspond to the trade of the pre-war
era, so that our exports would be the normal amount and we could
not be justly accused of being the principal foreign power to
assist Japan in her conquest of China.
Very sincerely yours,
J..
J. S. Burgess, Chairman
Department of Sociology
FILED
JSB:g

Hon. James J. Davis

whan things were getting ready to happen.

Senator Pittman was kind enough to intoduce a resolution condemm-
ing Japan and I see that the newspapers in New York interpret this as an indorse-N
ment of the State Department's policy in discouraging the sale of airplanes to
4
Japan. The Washington Post yesterday carried a story that the congregation of
the First Baptist Church in Washington passed a resolution indorsing the action
2
of the Senate and the First Baptist Church in New York passed a resolution indors
ing the resolution of the First Baptist Church in Washington, which indorsed the
resolution of Congress, which indorsed the action of the State Department.
Well, anyhow it seems as though the churches, peace societies
and others are now climbing aboard the band wagon. If Secretary Hull is encourag-
ing the people to make their foreign policy views kmowm to the State Department,
I am sure that the Secretary of State will be inundated during the next few months
with expressions of opinion favoring withdrawal of America's partnership with
Japan in the destruction of China.
I would like to hope that Mr. Libbey's six peace societies will
soon be faint voices crying in the wilderness but I fear that they are too well
versed in publicity and propaganda methods ever to make their voices very faint.
At the last session of the National Peace Conference in Washington you remember
that Mr. Libbey admitted that his group was now in the minority and appealed for
a compromise in order to avoid a split in that peace group. The compromise was
achieved but the point is that Libbey openly admitted that he was in the minority
although when he issues a statement to the public he still claims to speak for
the masses of the American people, which is rather stupid. The last time I visit-
6d his office in Washington I saw South Manchurian Railway calendars hanging prom-
inently in his private office and a large quantity of Japanese Chamber of Commerce.
and Tokyo Asahi literature cluttering the table in his waiting room. But therp
was no Chinese literature. Libbey is not anti-war he is out and out projzapanese.
However, I do not want to take your time with such triviata as
Libbey but I just wanted to write and express our appreciation for your courteay.
Very sincerely yours,
EarsH. Leaf.

Dear Sir:
I have been interested to read the recent addresses of yourself and
Assistant Secretary Sumner Welles with reference to the bombing of civilian
744.24
populations. I was particularly interested and pleased to rend in yesterday's
New York Times the statement to the effect that you woulc discourage the sale
of airplanes to Japan as long as such bombing continued.
Is it not permissible, however, even if not obligatory, to prohibit
the shipment to Japan directly or indirectly of gasoline and other munitions
or war supplies as well as airplanes. The Nine-Power Treaty provides that
the United States will "respect" the integrity of China and this Treaty if
executed by the United Stetes is part of the supreme law of the land. To
assist another power to destroy the integrity or China through furnishing it
with munitions for the accomplishment of that end seems to me a violation
of this enforceable obligation to "respect" such territorial integrity.
Yours respectfully
Kalph O. Wells

The Honorable Franklin D. Roosevelt

White House, Washington, D. C.
ver
 ro to the President
My dear Mr. President:
894.24/224
The brutal undeolared war which is being carried on by
Japan against China is deeply deplored by you, I am sure, as
it is by every peace loving American citizen.
If, however, the facts revealed in an article entitled
"Japan's Partner", which appeared in the June mmber of Harper's
magazine is correct, then this undeclared war is really made
possible by the war material and supplies being imported to
Japan from the United States.
This practice on our part is doubly regrettable, it seems
to me, in view of the fact that we are building up a huge navy,
which is purported to be for the purpose of defending the United
States against these "terrible Japanese" who, according to our
navy experts, will soon attack our shores.
Is it possible, Mr. President, that the United States is
Japan's partner in this undeolared war? Has not the time come
for our government to take drastio steps in enforoing the
neutrality law, forbidding shipment of war materials to Japan?
In the name of humanity and for the sake of the good
name of our beloved country use your high office to put a stop
to such inhuman and inconsistent polioy on the part of our
F/A
government.
Very respoctfully yours,
Geraes Waiams
GW:JB

June 23, 1938.
Right Reverend G. Aahton Oldham,
Bishop of Albany,
Albany, New York.
My dear Bishop Oldham:
I have just read with interest and hope your letter
in the New York Times of June 14 on the subjeot of
Japan's horror against China--interest in your compre-
hensive analysis and hope that your high oharacter and
wide influence may be the means of ending that tragedy.
You properly oharaoterize us as party with Japan
in her inhuman assault; for if our furnishing of these
supplies, with knowledge of the use to which they are to
be put does not so constitute us, then our peniten-
tiaries are peopled with innocent inmates whose only
offense was to hand to some one else the gun with which
to commit murder.
Further, in so furnishing these supplies we direotly
and affirmatively violate our engagement to China under
the Nine Power Treaty, in which we obligate ourselves:
(1) To respect the sovereignty, the independence,
and the territorial and administrative
integrity of China;
(2)
To provide the fullest and most unembarrassed
opportunity to China to develop and maintain
for herself an effeotive and stable government.
Add to this that "A treaty stands on the same footing
of supremaoy as do the provisions of the Constitution and
the laws of the United States. It operates of itself,
without the aid of any legislation, state or national."
(Asakura v. Seattle, 265 U.8. 332.) "Courts, when called
upon

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
upon to aot, should be oareful to see that international
engagements are faithfully kept and observed." (Sullivan
v. Kidd, 254 U.S. 433.); and (The President) "shall take
oare that the laws be fad thfully exeouted." (U. S. Con-
stitution, Art. II, Sec. 3), and we have here pointed
out to us the method by which we can stop Japan and thus
answer a fervent world prayer.
In other words, the fact that we are party to this
treaty gives us the unique opportunity, if not indeed
imposing upon us the duty, to stop these supplies 1n
restraint of our own misoonduct, rather than through
embargo, acousation or other possible war provocative
against Japan.
Nor is 1t needful or important to determine or
deolare that a state of war exists in China (which
deolaration, I understand, would operate against China).
The ohallenge to this treaty-guaranteed "sovereignty"
and "integrity" and "opportunity" is complete, and our
supplying of thesessentials makes our partioipation in
the ohallenge patent and indisputable.
Merely, let the President proclaim his reason to
believe that these supplies are being used in a physioal
assault against China, and directing that these shipments
be held up pending investigation of whether they oon-
stitute our violation of our obligations under the treaty.
Shall we take action on our own initiative (such as
I have suggested or otherwise) or are we to confess to a
complete poverty of ingenuity in statesmanship essential
to end this monstrous orime against humanity and the
sanotity of this treaty, and to square ourselves with
world righteousness and our own consoience?
You do well in your letter to oall attention also to
the "potential enemy" we are helping through these sup-
plies to build up; for with Japan's success in China she
will become not alone a military and naval world threat,
but economio as well--and each being a war breeder. with
China's almost wageless workers regimented in the manu-
facture of that country's vast raw materials into produots
of common demand throughout the world (1noluding this
country), our own labor will be reduced to a ohoice
between unemployment, md degradation to Oriental
standards of wages and living.
The recent congressional debate on the super-navy
bill developed the faot that fear of Japan was the moving
oause

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
cause for this wild and financially ruinous world naval
construotion race. with Japan's grandiose ambitions of
conquest her success or failure in China 1s more than
likely to spell the differenoe between world war in the
immediate, or peace for an indefinite, future.
A prinoiple of demooracy is that government shall
be responsive to the popular will. I believe that a
great organization for righteousness suoh as yours can
lead the way in an overwhelming publio expression to
whioh our administration will willingly give heed.
You are at liberty to make use of any material in
this letter, and to call upon me for cooperation in the
oause.
Very sinoerely,
FRED H. LYSONS

124
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The June seventeenth issue of the Bulletin of the
894.24/22
Foreign Policy Association states the fact that the United
States is supplying high-test aviation fuel to Japan in
large and increasing quantities, and has sold $5,670,237
worth of American aircraft and accessories to Japan since
January, 1938.
874.24
It goes on to say: "The United States, as the chief
source of supply of Japan's war materials, has become a
2
silent partner in Japanese aggression. It is not enough
6
to say that this country is also supplying munitions to
China. Nor does application of the Neutrality Act commend
itself as the proper answer to the situation that has de-
veloped. As early as last September the American govern-
ment explicitly ranged itself with the League in condemna-
tion of Japan's bombing of open cities in China; in more
recent declarations, the State Department has implicitly
branded Japan as the aggressor in the current Far Eastern
hostilities. Something more than words is required, however,
if the United States is not to be accused of transparent
hypocrisy."
It seems to me that these facts are an outrage to the
moral conscience of America. Cannot something be done to
make America no longer particeps criminis with Japan in the
present assault on China?
F
Sincerely yours,
A
W.Juxeee
W. Russell Bowie
Rector of Grace Church

ALMIGHTY GOD), Father of all mercies. we thine unworthy servants do give thee
most humble and hearty thanks for all thy goodness and loving kindness to us, and
to all men: We b'ess thee for our creation. preservation. and all the blessings of
this life: but al ve all. for thine inestimable love in the redemption of the world
by our Lord Jesu Christ for the means of grace, and for the hope of glory. And
we beseech thee. yive us that due sense oi all thy mereies, that our hearts may be
unfeignedly tharkful. and that we shew forth thy praise, not only with our lips.
but in our lives: by giving up ourselves to thy service and by walking before thee
in holiness and righteousness all our days: through Jesus Christ our Lord, to

• The offering this evening will go toward defraying expenses of the Institute of Inter-
national Relaton
Church-time Care of Children. Boys and girls under 9 years of age will
be cared for in the Kindergarten Room, Pilgrim Hall, under the direc-
tion of Miss Virginia Rathbun.
The Church Library in Pilgrim Hall is always open for at least half an
hour following the morning service, and upon request at any time dur-
ing the week.
Morning and Evening Services broadcast from the church auditorium over
Station KRE, 1370 kilocycles.
Plymouth House, after its usual fellowship hour from 5 to 6 today, will
hear Rev. Randolph Miller, Chaplain of St. Mark' Epi copal church, on
"Conceptions of God in Modern Philosophy".
New Members will be received into our fellowship next Sunday, July 3.
The pastor will be glad to confer with any desiring to take this  tep.
It is a privilege, and a very happy one, for this church to have
as guest speaker in its pulpit Dr. T. Z. Koo, distinguished Christian
of China and of the world. Dr. Koo is presented under the auspices
of the Institute of International Relations, now in conference at
Mills College.
"We Are Changing the World!"
It is the bold chant of the youth of Soviet Russia, remindful of
great days when men of Christian faith and practice spoke like
that. Christian men'and women : till have the truth that can change
the world to nobleness, security, and peace, but every Sunday I am
sowing among you this insistence: We'll never do it unless we
achieve more conviction, cooperation and devotion. It would be the
Riddle's answer. Thin piping makes no rousing music. A thin jet
drowns no fire.
--The Pastor

Dear Sir:
On Monday evening, last, the 20th. inst., in
3
a meeting at Centenary Church of this City, where were
9
gathered the ministers of the Methodist Episcopal Church,
South, of the Chattanooga District, and their wives,
leading laymen and their wives, also of this District,
and others, in all representing approximately Twelve
Thousand (12,O00) Methodists, a set of resolutions was
adopted, and the writer, being the Chairman of the meet-
ing, was instructed to forward to you a copy of the reso-
lutions, which is enclosed herewith, together with the

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
THE SINO-JAPANESE WAR
Resolutions adopted at a meeting renresenting approxi-
mately Twleve Thousand (l2, ooo) Methodists of the Chattanooga
District, Methodist Episcopal Church, South:
WHEREAS, It is a matter of common knowledge that the territory of
China has been invaded by the armed forces of Japan and,
WHEREAS, It is reliably reported that muterials of war and other
supplies purchased in America are being used in inflict-
ing great suffering and loss of life and property upon
the people of China, therefore,
BE IT RESOLVED: That we call upon the President and Congress of
the United States to take such measures as may be neces-
sary to prevent the material and financial resources of
the government or citizens of this country from being
used, either directly or indirectly, to aid Japan in
her war of aggression upon China.

Dear Mr. President:
The undersigned citizens present in the congrega-
tion of the Calvary Methodist Churen, minneapolis, request
894.24/244
your consideration of the following resolution:
Whereas, our Government nas named Japan as the
aggressor nation in the present conflict in China, ana has
repeatedly protested against the bombings of undefended Chi-
nese cities, the brutal slaying of noncombatants, and the
destruction of missions and hospitals, nd,
Whereas, the anm ed forces of Japan have continued
to repeat these brutal acts, and
Whereas, ran me terials from our country are being
used to manufacture the Japenese suns, tanks, arnored cers, and
other weapons of var, American bombing plans are being used to
carry Ame rican-made bombs for the destruction of Chinese civil-
ian populations, American shells are loading the Japanese guns
and American credits are helping to finence Japan's ae ressign
in China,
We therefore protest the American policy wuicn makes
it possible for certain American interests to profit from the
blood and tears of the innocent victims of Japanese aggression,
and,
JUL 281938
We urge the Government of the United States to find
and use every lawful means to prevent the material and financial
FILED F/WB
resources of this country from being used eitner directly or
indirectly to aid Japan in her operations in China, and
We direct that copies of this resolution also be
sent to the Secretary of State, anu to our representatives in
Congress.
Respectfully submitted,

Paris, le  June 7,
1938
Dear Mr. Weeks:
I appreciate your letter of May llth which reminds me
of many pleasant memories at Columbia. In the present circum-
stances of my country it 1s a source of comfort and strength
to receive from time to time sympathetic letters like yours
from my erstwhile college mates.
The whole country is behind the policy of determined
resistance to the Japanese aggression and the morale of both
the people and the fighting services 1s excellent, and we are
confident of our ultimate victory.
with thanks and regards,
Yours sincerely
V. K. WELLINGTON KOO
Mr. C. P. Weeks,
5000 Walnut St.,
Phila, Pa.,
U.S.A.

894.24/254
1-1408
Mr. Hawk said that before returning to China to resume
mission work he proposed to make an extensive speaking tour
of the United States with a view to acquainting the American
people with the situation in the Far East and to promoting
1
public opinion favoring the stoppage of the shipment of
2
munitions to Japan. He said that he understood that 54 per
cent of all Japanese imports of war supplies came from the
United States and asked whether I could confirm this state-
ment. He asked my opinion also as to the advisability of
amending or repealing the Neutrality Act as well as my ad-
A
vice in regard to the most effective means of gaining pub-
lic support for the measures he advocated.
I told him that it would be difficult to state cate-
gorically the percentage of Japan's imports of war supplies
from

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
from the United States, as this would involve defining the
articles included in war supplies. I said that if he de-
sired statistical material regarding shipments to Japan or
China of specifiderticles I would endeavor to obtain them
for him, and that it was my understanding, with: regard to
articles for which an export license was required in this
country, thet up to the present shipments to China exceeded
those to Japan. I observed that I could not give him any
advice in regard to the advisability of repealing the
Neutrality Act as this was a matter lying within the prov-
ince of the legisletive branch of the Government. I said
that in a democracy such as our own a private citizen had
a perfect right to discuss public questions and to acquaint
the American people with matters within his knowledge re-
garding the situation in the Far East. I suggested, however,
that in regard to the matter of how he might most effectively
present his views to the American people he should seek the
advice of persons more experienced than myself on questions
of public relations.
Mr. Hawk appeared satisfied with his interview and
appreciative of my offer to supply him with any statistical
data he might ask for.
. W.B.

My dear Judge Hull:
Rry-Fme
I am writing you as one who has been a warm dmirer of yourself
894.24/256
and your handling of your great office ever since you beceme Secretary of State.
I have rejoiced in your growing popularity and prestige throughout our whole
country. The trade arrengements you have been making with various nations are
a powerful factor in promoting international peace.
However, my dear Judge Hull, there is one thing I should like to
bring to your attention most definitely. As one who travels widely throughout
our country, I want to tell you that there is e rising and practically universal
tide of indignation at the way our country is supplying Japan with munitions of
war which she uses to conmit wholesale murders throughout China. In the Foreig
FILED
Policy Bulletin of July 15, we read these words, "Only one measure can alter thil
3
prospect of continued devastation in China - refusal of the Western powers, par-
ticularly the United States, to continue furnishing war supplies to the aggressor."
In a letter just received from one of our ablest missionaries, sta-
tioned at Haichow, North Kiangsu, China, he reports continual bombings of that
F/WB
city for the last two weeks. "Some days," he writes, "there is hardly any time at
all left between the raids. By the time one raid is over, before there is time for
the all-clear signal, another group of planes is often in sight, and so it goes day
by day." He also writes in this letter dated June 6 as follows:-
"Sometimes I look up while a bombing raid is going on, and

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.98)
dell Hull - 2
prey 'C Lord, how long,' and at other times the cuestion insistently
comes up, 'Whet has becone of the conscience of the word, especially
of the so-celled Christian nations?' Such slauhter of helpless men,
women, and children, when no military adventg e can be gained by it,
often prectically destroying towns and villages which have no soldiers
in them at all, and also destroying large arts of such places as Hai-
chow, hardly inflicting one casualty amon the soldiers, is simply
unthinkable. I do not believe the Japanese people know what is being
done. I do not believe the responsible military leaders, even, know
what is being done by the reiders of the eir. Their reports all state
that they have bombed great militery concentrations. Several times I
have heard them say over the radio that they have just bombed great
military concentrations in Haichow end vicinity. If they only knew it,
most of their terrible raids have not touched a single soldier."
Such quotations from our missionaries I could multiply indefinitely,
showing that whole villages where there sre no soldiers or military euuipment at
all are being ruthlessly wiped out with the wholesale murder of women, children,
batants
and other non-com s. I trust you will not consider it an intrusion if I say
that there is a fundemental inconsistency, not to say iniouity, in our country's
furnishing Japan, an unquestioned aggressor nation, with the materiel resources
with which she is devasteting the Chinese people, while et the same time our
people are urged to contribute to relieve the suffering thus caused in China.
I em sure, Mr. Secretary, that I voice the feelings of the vast
majority of the Americen people in earnestly requesting you to teke some step
that will discontinue America's partnership with Japan in bringing these untold
miseries upon the Chinese people.
With greet respect and best wishes, I am
Earttt
Cordielly yours,

FAH EASTERN AFFAIRS

Mr. Cordell Hull

Secretary of State,

State Department,
Washington D.C.
Dear Mr. Hull,
Though I am only eighteen years old, and so am
894.24/261
not a voter, I feel that it is my duty and the duty
of other young people to protest against the ship-
ment of oil, scrapiron, and airplanes from the
United States to Japan. These materials are essential
to the Japanese if they wish to continue their war
whith China, and since most people in America desire
world peace, why is it that this shipping is not dis-
continued?
Many people feel that war in Japan and China
is a threat to our own peace. But even if this is
not so, must America stand by and see the people of
China massacred with war materials that come from
our own country?
Surely the State Department could bring pressure
to bear on the companies which export oil and scrap-
iron and those who are sending airplanes to Japan, if
only in the form of telling them of the many people
who protest against it. If these exportations were
stopped, the Chino-Japanese war would certainly be
brought to a close more quickly.
Sincerely yours,
Helen Bell
Y

My dear Mr. Hull:
DeparyedState
An increasing number of Church leaders are seriously
disturbed by the attitude of our Government in reference to the
sale of mmitions and other war supplies to Japan. We are of the
opinion that an embargo should be placed on all war supplies to
Japan so leng as this nation continues her undeolared war against
894.24/263
China. We are of this opinion because of the ruthless, barbarous
and wajustifiable procedure that has marked the effort of the
militaristio party in Japan to destroy China.
We readily agree that we do not have access to the informa-
tion which has come to you in your high position. We have every
confidence in you as a citizen of Tennessee and the ranking officer
in the Cabinet. However, we are convinced that the right-thinking
people of the United States are finding it difficult to justify our
attitude toward China in view of the attitude which has been main-
tained with reference to Spain.
I am sure that you will give this commumication such
consideration as in your opinion it merits.
Most cordially yours,
k Duilli

TAH EASTEHN AFFAIRS
Dear Mr. President:--
cou
894.24/271
As a group of ladies vitally interested in human welfare thruout the
entire world. We the Early Bible Class of Bassett Memorial Methodist
Church, are protesting to you, Mr. President, asthe highest authority
in our nation, regarding the indifferent attitude of our Government
in allowing the shipment of War Materials to Japan, the aggressor in
the Japan-China War.
While we, as a group, are not acquainted with all the fine points of the
"Neutrality Act", we do feel that our U. S. Government is using the excuse
"that Japan has not declared war," as a mere pretext to allow shipment of
Munitions Materials solely for the Monetary Profit to the Government and
individual corporations.
And so, we urge you, Mr. President, to use your influence as Chief Ex-
ecutive, and the authority you ask for and received under the"Neutrality
Act," to prohibit this shipment of Munitions to any aggressor in time of War.
F/WB
A little less diplomacy and a lot more of Christ's principles for living,
would go far toward promoting peace and good will with all nations.

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.98)
Christianity as well as Democracy, is on trial the world over, and we would truly
like to see Christ's way of life used as a guide in both the Foreign and Domestic
Policies of our beloved United States, so as to point the way for otherNations
who do not enjoy the leadership and prestige that our Owm Mation enjoys.
Assuring you of our interest and prayers, that you may have Divine Guidance and
great courage to meet the problems of Chief Executive,
We are sincerely yours,,
THE EARLY BIBLE CLASS
(mos.) myrtle cae. Dick
Secretary.

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
Rosolutionsadopted by the Ministers and Laymen in at-
tendance upon the Bible Conference of Religion and Life
at Lakeside, Ohio, July 23, 1938.
Wheras, Japan is waging an undeclared war against China, and,
has 1n this warfare practiced wanton destruction, resorting to
unprovoked attacks upon civilian population, especially by op-
eration of air forces, we in America are increasingly feeling
the inconsistency of supplying munitions of war to Japan to be
used in these ways.
Therefore BE IT RESOLVED, that we want it clearly understood
that the Christian people of America are positively against such
an act of aggression as is being carried on by Japan, and,
That while we do not favor military interference, we recommend
and urge, in the interest of humanity and peace, that war material
be withheld and economic credit in all its forms be withdrawn from
Japan, and,
That we express our deep sympathy for the population of both
China and Japan, who doubtless are suffering under conditions
over which they have no control.
We desire that copies of these resolutions be sent to the
Secretary of State, the Hon. Cordell Hull, and to the Senators
and Congressmen from the State of Ohio.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
COMLLONS

Departmrnt of Stats
ack
Dear Mr. Secretary:
RrH-RMe
As your official auties make you responsible in
a great degree for the foreign policies of our governement , I address
you as a common citizen concerning those policies, and especially
that policy adopted by our government in relationship to Far Eastern
affairs.
The undeclared war in China has now been in progress
gor over one year, and during that year your department has sent to
89
the Nipponese Emperor's statesmen several notes in dignified terms
expressing official disappoval of numerous nefarious acts that the
4
Japanese have committed from time to time. Your notes attempting to
dissuade the Japanese from ruthlessly bombing the civilan population
are to be commended. But after studying the situation one is forced to
2
doubt the sincerity of your interest in promoting peace in the Far East.
4
The Neutrality Act which if enforced could do much
2
to terminate this ruthless aggrandizement on the part of the Japanese
7
has been neglected as if it did not exist. Of course one realizes that
9
were it invoked in Japan's case, it wo ld have to apply to the Spanish
situation equally, with the result that France, Italy, and hussia -
practically all Europe - would come under your official censorship. That
of course would be contrary to your policy of trying to keep friendly
with those powers with whom you have concluded or with hom you hope
to conclude trade pacts, and is easily understood.
But the Chinese situation is different, Japan is trying
to establish a life line on the continent of Asia at the expense of
a nation desperately struggling to establish its netional and racial
integrity. A great . any Americans have extracted from China vast wealth
without contributing anything to its welfare. I mention specifically
the British - American Tobacco Company, whioh is now co-operating with
the Japanese in the Shanghai area, and have co-operated with puppet
Manchukuo ever since it was made a Japnese protected state, and the
Standard Oil Company, whihh has always cast its lot with the potential
victor. True the Rockefeller interests did give Peking Union Medical
College, but raised the price of kerosene the next day after its
opening, and is now along with other of our large oil companies furnigh-
ing the fuel that makes it possible for unheralded and sudden death to
sweep from the sky like  hungry vulture on innocent non- combatants.
It seems to me that our State Department , if it is
honest and has no seoret agreements with our manufacturers of war
materials, could make kmown its official disapproval of exporting these
imstruementa of warfare to Japans without enforoing the Neutrality Aot.
Amy clamor on the part of Japan be met by an official note, similar to

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.96)
to those very polite and beautifully worded epistles that Japan , herself
sends out to those she insults on so meny occasions, and then officially
considers the matter closed.
Perhaps I as a humble sitizen no nothing about the problems
that confront those who guide the destinies of our government, and that
this letter is pure effrontery. However, it seems to me that diplomacy
no matter how scholerly and tactful is nothing les chicanery, when it
does not make a whole hearted attempt to use all the weapons that are
at its disposal to hasten the close of a war that is as destructive as
was the Thirty Years War in Europe. We have witnessed the desecration,
destruation and rape of Spain, can we call ourselves civilized or
humanitarian if we allow the same thing to happen in Asia without
using all our influence to forestall such a calamity?
Very truly yours,
(min) E l h ceomen

THE SINOJAPANKSE WAR.

has been invaded by the armed forces of Japan and,

purchased in America are being used in inflicting great suffer-

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.88)
17
RESOLUTION
We, the faculty and students of the Old Salem
Epworth League Institute of the Illinois Conference of the
Metnodist Episcopal Church, deplore the present slaughter
and destruction of life in Chine among civilians and mil-
itary forces alike.
We recognize and regret the part which our nation
has had in teaching westerr methcds of warfare to both China
and Japan and in providing implements and munitions which
are being used in the present conflict in the Orient.
We feel that the present armament policy of our
nation is detrimental to the future peace of the world and
to the well-being of our own nation.
We believe that war is raver the best means of
settling problems and differences between nations.
Therefore, we request that immediate action be
taken by the President and Congress of the United States
as follows:
1. To reduce the tremendous expenditures for
armaments within our own nation.
2. To stop the shipment of munitions of war
such as gasoline, oil, scrap iron, airplanes, ammunition,
etc. to the Orient.

=== Page 546 === (confidence 0.87)
RESOLUTION
We, the faculty and students of the Old Salem
Epworth League Institute of the Illinois Conference of the
Methodist Episcopal Church, deplore the present slaughter
and destruction of life in China among civilians and mil-
itary forces alike.
We recognize and regret the part which our nation
has had in teaching western methods of warfare to both China
and Japan and in providing implements and munitions which
are being used in the present conflict in the Orient.
We feel that the present armament policy of our
nation is detrimental to the future peace of the world and
to the well-being of our own nation.
We believe that war is never the best means of
settling problems and differences between nations.
Therefore, we request that immediate action be
taken by the President and Congress of the United States
as follows:
1. To reduce the tremendous expenditures for
armaments within our own nation.
2. To stop the shipment of munitions of war
such as gasoline, oil, scrap iron, airplanes, ammunition,
etc. to the Orient.

Dear Mr. President:
RECORDS
On behalf of the pastors and laymen of the
Atlanta Conference of the Methodist Episcopal Church representing
a membership and constituency of more than 25,000, we the
undersigned District Superintendents of the Atlanta Conference
desire to register our protest against the continued shipment of
arms and munitions of war to Japan. We, with the rest of the
civilized world, have been shocked by the wanton destruction of
894.24/282
the lives of thousands of women, children and other non-combatents.
We respectfully ask that you use your good
offices to halt the shipment of war materials to Japan.
Respectfully submitted,

=== Page 615 === (confidence 0.97)
No. 232
AMERICAN CONSULATE GENERAL
Hong Kong, July 11th, 1938
DT
894.24
SUBJECT:
Eugene Chen's Criticism of the Supply
of American War Materials to Japan.
57
THE HONORABLE
B
THE SECRETARY OF STATE
FAR EASTEAN AFFAIRS
CO
MAUG 9-1938
WASHINOTON
Department of 
894.24/28
SIR
I have the honor to quote the following statement
made lately by Mr. Eugene Chen to a representative of
the United Press in Hong Kong:
5
"Japanese guns and aeroplanes, some
of which have been supplied by American
manufacturers, have killed and wounded over
a mill1on Chinese and flung another 150,000,000
Fb
into misery and tribulation.
"This work of death and misery will go
on as long as the people of the United States
un
continue to assist the Japanese to carry on
the war and the Government of the United States
continue to disregard their treaty obligations
towards China.
"By buying Japanese goods and selling
arms and ammunition and granting credit
FILED
facilities to the Japanese, the American
people, bankers, manufacturers, women, and
children, since they too buy Japanese products,
F/WB
are objectively assisting this lawless invasion
of a country whose people chose to turn west
for security and for co-operation in peace and
war, on the faith of the treaty promises of
the United States and other powers."
Mr. Eugene Chen, whose past history and activities
in China are well known to the Department, arrived in
Hong Kong from Europe several weeks ago. There has been
much

=== Page 616 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
much speculation here as to his future plans and
ambitions. The consensus of opinion is that he has
been hoping to procure nomination to a position of
importance - and profit - in either the Central Chinese
Government or the Canton Government, but has, so far,
had little definite encouragement. He has been living
more or less quietly in Kowloon apparently advising
his two sons, Percy and Jack, who spend considerable
time in Canton prospecting the situation there in his
behalf. Percy, who maintains a home in Hong Kong (Kow-
loon) with his attractive Communist Russian wife, im-
presses me as an unusually energetic and capable young
man. His command of English, both written and spoken,
is admirable and he has been doing some propaganda and
publicity work which he has informed me is officially
for the Chinese Government and for which he is paid by
1t.
British intelligence officials tend to the opinion
that Father Eugene's more or less quiet life and demeanor
in Hong Kong to date do not conform to his usual inclina-
tions for the lime-light and are therefore somewhat
mystifying. They suspect that he is only biding his
time for an opportunity "to stir up" something hectic or
exciting in its effects or possibilities. Accordingly
the above quotation might indicate at least one of his
thought trends which are very likely aimed at finding
a way actively to reenter either the Chinese or the
international political situation. Presumably the near
futuro

=== Page 617 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
future will give a clearer indication of Mr. Chen's
intentions or ambitions, in which event this office
will submit further comment.
Very respectfully,
Addison E. Southard
American Consul General
In quintuplicato to the Department
Copy to Embassy, Hankow
Copy to Embassy, London
800.
AES/mm

919
Washington, D.C.
RUH/
Dear Sir,
Contemplating return to our former home in Peiping ,
China, we are filled with consternation at the prospect of haf-
ing to explain the part our country has taken in supplying
the Japanese with war materials for their aggression on China.
We shall be reminded that up to date we are said to have sup
plied 54% of the materials used by Japan in this war, and
therefore stand morally responsible for half thed eaths and
894.24/2
suffering caused to our traditional friends the Chinese peo-
ple. Such moral responsibility of the arms merchant has been
taught to the Chinese by their sages from of old and 1s in-
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
wrought in the thought and feelings of the people.
6
There have beenreports that the munition makers are
extending credit to Japan for a part of the materials sup-
plied. Is it not true that the Department of State is able
to discourage or prevent that and also has precedent for so
doing?.
It may be that we have overlooked it, but we have
never seenreference to the joint resolution introduced intoro
6
Congress, Dec. 20, 1937, by Hon. Byron N. Scott of Californi
having been brought up for hearing by the Committee on Foreign
Affairs, to which it was referred. Whether there has been a
hearing or not, it is in line with the action that we would
like to s ee our country take in order to be consistent with
friendly statements that have been made by our President and
other officiala.

=== Page 633 === (confidence 0.98)
2
It would seem to be high time, before we losethe confi-
dence of the Chinese people in our friendship, and before
they may be ovorwhelmed, that something be done to stop the
sales and extension of credit to Japan and to give material
aid to China. We sincerely believe that this will be for the
good of both peoples.
It is to be hoped that something may be done during the
next few months, that will enable Americans to argue with
their Chinese friends the essential friendship of our peo-
ple and of our government. After much travel in this coun-
try we have yet to hear anyting but sympathy and friendly
expressions for the Chinese among our people. Our government
ought to reflect this attitude in its action.
George D. Wilder s Hertrde S. Nildir.
Yours Respectfully
Missionary of the American Board of Commission-

My dear Mr. Schorr:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of

mation be supplied you in regard to American and other
foreign loans or credits which have been extended to
China and Japan since the beginning of hcstilities be-
tween those two countries and in regard to American and
British investments in China and Japan.
The Denartment is not aware that any loans have
been obtnined in the United States by either Japanese or
Chinese borrowers sine the beginning of hostilities in
July 1937. With regard to your question "To what uxtent
have American industrialists . . . sent equipment to
Japan . . .", I assume thot you refer to cquipment ship-
ped on credit. As you doubtless know, the financing of
the movement of exports and imports between countries
is normally effectid through banking accommodations in
the form of commercial credits. The Department has no
information in regard to credits which may have been
obtained by Japonese borrowers in the United States
other than credits of the nature mertioned. Neither
does it have information in regard to the extent to which
British, Russian, Germon or Italian interests may have
extunded loans or credits to China or Jopan since the
beginning of hostilities.
The latest comprehensive official figures of Ameri-
con investments in China and Japan are thosc published
in the "Balance of Internationai Payments of the United
States in 1933", pages 53-62. According to this publicn-
tion American business investments in China in 1933
amounted to $132,000,000, f wh1ch $125,000,000 consisted
of direct investments and $7,000,000 of portfolin invest-
ments, while in Japan American direct investments amounted
to $60,000,000 and portf~115 1nvestments to $358,000,000,
